Singular limits of anistropic weak solutions to compressible magnetohydrodynamics
Nicolas Besse, Christophe Cheverry

Abstract
. The aim is to justify rigorously the so-called reduced magnetohydrodynamic model (abbreviated as RMHD), which is widely used in fusion, space and astrophysical plasmas. Motivated by physics, the focus is on plasmas that are simultaneously strongly magnetized and anisotropic. We consider conducting fluids that can be described by viscous and resistive barotropic compressible magnetohydrodynamic equations. The purpose is to study the asymptotic behaviour of global weak solutions, which do exist, for strongly anisotropic plasmas such as the large aspect ratio framework. We prove that such anisotropic weak solutions converge to the weak solutions of the RMHD equations. Rigorous justification of this limit is performed both in a periodic domain and in the whole space. It turns out that the resulting system is incompressible only in the perpendicular direction to the external strong magnetic field, whereas it involves compressible features in the parallel direction. In order to pass to the singular limit in the perpendicular direction we exploit, among others, tools elaborated for proving the low Mach number limit of compressible fluid flows such as the introduction of a fast oscillatory unitary group associated to the dynamics of transverse fast magnetosonic waves. In the parallel direction, we bring out compactness arguments and particular cancellations coming from the structure of our equations.



†
†
footnotetext:
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.

Keywords.
Parabolic systems of conservation laws; Singular limit of nonlinear PDEs; Magnetohydrodynamics; Fusion plasmas;
Weak solutions of dissipative PDEs; Large magnetic field; Spatial anisotropy.

1.
Introduction

Equations of reduced magnetohydrodynamics, hereafter abbreviated as RMHD, are extensively used in fusion, space and astrophysical plasmas. They are highly prized by plasma physicists for the following reasons. First, they allow interesting theoretical and analytical developments; second they are the source of numerically tractable models which are used to bring forth codes that are routinely exploited
[
3
,
10
,
31
,
35
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
. The RMHD model was introduced in the seventhies
[
26
,
44
]
in the context of fusion plasmas. It was followed by many systematic studies and generalizations including more and more physical effects and refinements
[
12
,
13
,
22
,
24
,
29
,
31
,
45
,
47
,
48
]
. At present, there is a vast literature about formal derivations and applications of RMHD models. The two references
[
4
,
40
]
are good introductions to the subject, with many references. RMHD equations are still a very active research field, including recent progress in extended magnetohydrodynamics
[
1
]
. At the same time, the work
[
2
]
has highlighted the importance and the inherent difficulties of working under anisotropic conditions.

1.1.
The penalized system

Let
\Omega
be a three-dimensional domain, which is either the periodic box
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}:=({\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}})^{3}
or the whole space
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. The time evolution on
\Omega
of plasmas is basically described by isentropic compressible magnetohydrodynamics. The unknowns are made of the fluid density
\uprho\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
, the fluid velocity
\text{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and the magnetic field
\text{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Including viscous (
\upmu>0
and
\uplambda>0
) and resistive (
\upeta>0
) effects, we consider on
\mathbb R_{+}\times\Omega
the system of MHD equations

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\text{t}}\uprho+\nabla\cdot(\uprho\,\text{v})=0\,,\\
&\partial_{\text{t}}(\uprho\,\text{v})+\nabla\cdot(\uprho\,\text{v}\otimes\text{v})+\nabla\text{p}+\text{B}\times(\nabla\times\text{B})-\upmu\,\Delta\text{v}-\uplambda\,\nabla(\nabla\cdot\text{v})=0\,,\\
&\partial_{\text{t}}\text{B}+\nabla\times(\text{B}\times\text{v})-\upeta\,\Delta\text{B}=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

(1)

together with the divergence-free condition
\nabla\cdot\text{B}=0
and the barotropic state law
\text{p}=\mathfrak{a}\uprho^{\gamma}
, where
\mathfrak{a}>0
and
\gamma>1
. The symbol
\times
denotes the cross product of vectors of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, while the symbol
\otimes
stands for the tensor product of vectors. Given
(n,m)\in{\mathbb{N}}_{*}^{2}
, we take the convention

\big(\nabla\cdot(u\otimes v)\big)_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}(u_{i}v_{j})\,,\quad u={}^{t}(u_{1},\cdots,u_{m})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\,,\quad v={}^{t}(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,.

Following preceding results about compressible Navier–Stokes equations
[
15
,
32
]
, the existence of global (in time) weak solutions to system (
1
) has been obtained in
[
23
]
. Now, most plasmas are magnetized. This means that
B
is, in a first rough approximation, a given external non-zero magnetic field
\text{B}_{\mathbbl{e}}
. For simplicity, we assume that
\text{B}_{\mathbbl{e}}
is constant. After rotation, it can always be adjusted in such a way that
\text{B}_{\mathbbl{e}}=\mathbbmss{B}\,e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
with
\mathbbmss{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{*}
and
e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}:={}^{t}(0,0,1)
. The direction
e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
is called
parallel
. Given a vector field like
B
(or
v
), we can decompose
B
into its parallel component
\text{B}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\text{B}_{3}:=\text{B}\cdot e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and its perpendicular component
\text{B}_{\perp}:={}^{t}(\text{B}_{1},\text{B}_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
so that
\text{B}={}^{t}({}^{t}\text{B}_{\perp},\text{B}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
. We work away from vacuum, near a constant density which can always be put in the form
\mathbbmss{B}^{2}\,\bbrho
for some
\bbrho\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{*}
. Observe that
(\mathbbmss{B}^{2}\,\bbrho,0,\text{B}_{\mathbbl{e}})
is a constant solution to (
1
). Motivated by physics, particularly by considerations of large aspect ratio and geometrical optics (see Section
3
), we incorporate a strong spatial anisotropy. More precisely, we keep
\text{x}_{\perp}:=(\text{x}_{1},\text{x}_{2})=x_{\perp}=(x_{1},x_{2})
and we replace the vertical direction by
x_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=x_{3}:=\varepsilon\,\text{x}_{3}
with
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
. The above gradient operator becomes

\nabla_{\varepsilon}:={}^{t}({}^{t}\nabla_{\perp},0)+\varepsilon\,\nabla_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\,,\qquad\nabla_{\perp}:={}^{t}(\partial_{1},\partial_{2})\,,\qquad\nabla_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}:=e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\,\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\,,\qquad\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\equiv\partial_{3}\,.

(2)

Accordingly, a distinction must be drawn between
\Delta_{\perp}:=\partial^{2}_{11}+\partial^{2}_{22}
and
\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}:=\partial^{2}_{33}
. We want to study the behavior at large time scales
t:=\varepsilon\,\text{t}\sim 1
of small perturbations, of size
\varepsilon
, of the stationary solution
(\mathbbmss{B}^{2}\,\bbrho,0,\text{B}_{\mathbbl{e}})
. To this end, we seek solutions in the form

(\uprho,\text{v},\text{B})(\text{t},\text{x})=\bigl(\mathbbmss{B}^{2}\,\rho^{\varepsilon},\,\varepsilon\,v^{\varepsilon},\,\mathbbmss{B}\,(e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\varepsilon\,B^{\varepsilon})\bigr)(\varepsilon\text{t},{x}_{1},\text{x}_{2},\varepsilon\text{x}_{3})\,.

The unknowns are now
(\rho^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon})(t,x)
, while the pressure is given by
p^{\varepsilon}=a\,(\rho^{\varepsilon})^{\gamma}
, with
a=\mathbbmss{B}^{2(\gamma-1)}\mathfrak{a}
. Then, the system (
1
) can be reformulated according to the following equations

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\rho^{\varepsilon}+\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot(\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon})=0\,,\\
&\partial_{t}(\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon})+\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot(\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}\otimes v^{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\,\nabla_{\varepsilon}{p}^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\varepsilon B^{\varepsilon})\times(\nabla_{\varepsilon}\times B^{\varepsilon})\\
&\qquad\quad\ \,-\mu_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\perp}v^{\varepsilon}-\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v^{\varepsilon}-\lambda^{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\varepsilon}(\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot v^{\varepsilon})=0\,,\\
&\partial_{t}B^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\varepsilon}\times\big((e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\varepsilon B^{\varepsilon})\times v^{\varepsilon}\big)-\eta_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\perp}B^{\varepsilon}-\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

(3)

together with

\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot B^{\varepsilon}=0\,.

(4)

Without loss of generality, just to simplify the presentation, we can work with
\bbrho=1
. The second equation (for the momentum
\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}
) in (
3
) indicates that
\rho^{\varepsilon}
should be like
\rho^{\varepsilon}=\bbrho+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
. With this in mind, we can introduce the new state variable
\varrho^{\varepsilon}
as indicated below, and expand
{p}^{\varepsilon}
in powers of
\varepsilon
to obtain

\rho^{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon\,\varrho^{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad{p}^{\varepsilon}=a+\varepsilon\,\mathbbmssit{p}^{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\,,\qquad\mathbbmssit{p}^{\varepsilon}:=b\,\varrho^{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad b:=a\,\gamma\,.

(5)

To see the heuristics which lead to our model, it is instructive to interpret (
3
) in terms of
(\varrho^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon})
, and then to extract the singular part. We find

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\varrho^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\nabla_{\perp}\cdot v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\mathcal{O}(1)\,,\\
&\partial_{t}v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\nabla_{\perp}(\mathbbmssit{p}^{\epsilon}+B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})=\mathcal{O}(1)\,,\qquad\partial_{t}v^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\mathcal{O}(1)\,,\\
&\partial_{t}B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\nabla_{\perp}\cdot v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}(1)\,,\qquad\qquad\partial_{t}B^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\mathcal{O}(1)\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(6)

According to the terminology of Schochet
[
42
]
, the asymptotic regime is called:

•

slow
when the first-order time derivative of the solution remains bounded uniformly with respect to the small paramater
\varepsilon
(as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
). In view of (
6
), this means that

\nabla_{\perp}\cdot v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,,\qquad\nabla_{\perp}(\mathbbmssit{p}^{\epsilon}+B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,.

(7)

•

fast
when it is not slow. In this case, rapid oscillations with non-vanishing amplitudes can persist on a long time scale, preventing the convergence in a usual strong sense.
Since the singular part involves the sole action of the operator
\nabla_{\perp}
, it induces a propagation which can only be achieved with respect to the perpendicular direction. Then, because Alfvén waves do not propagate in the directions orthogonal to the ambiant magnetic field (here
e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
), we are necessarily concerned with transverse fast magnetosonic waves. This claim is justified in Section
3.1
, where the eigenmodes of the linear (singular) system (
6
) are investigated.

At time
t=0
, we start with

\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\mid t=0}=\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad v^{\varepsilon}_{\mid t=0}=v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad B^{\varepsilon}_{\mid t=0}=B_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,.

(8)

In coherence with (
4
), we must impose
\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot B_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0
. The initial data is said to be
prepared
when

\nabla_{\perp}\cdot v^{\varepsilon}_{0\perp}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,,\qquad\nabla_{\perp}(b\,\varrho^{\epsilon}_{0}+B^{\varepsilon}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}})=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,.

(9)

At this stage, it should be noted that the structure of the penalized terms inside (
6
) and of the subsequent condition (
7
) are different from isotropic situations
[
25
]
: the fluid should be almost incompressible only in the perpendicular direction (the action of the operator
\nabla_{\perp}
appears in place of the full gradient
\nabla
); the components
\varrho^{\epsilon}_{0}
and
B^{\varepsilon}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}
must be approximately linked together. To our knowledge, the asymptotic study of systems like (
3
) has not yet been undertaken neither in a smooth context or for weak solutions.

From now on, we assume that the positive perpendicular and parallel shear viscosities
\mu_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}>0
and
\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}>0
, as well as the positive bulk viscosity
\lambda^{\varepsilon}>0
are adjusted in such a way that

\mu_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\mu_{\perp}>0\,,\quad\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}>0\,,\quad\lambda^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\lambda>0\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0_{+}\,.

(10)

Similarly the positive perpendicular and parallel resistivities
\eta_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}>0
and
\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}>0
must satisfy

\eta_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\eta_{\perp}>0\,,\quad\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}>0\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0_{+}\,.

(11)

The system (
3
) is equipped with a conserved energy. We mainly assume that the energy of the initial data
(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon},v_{0}^{\varepsilon},B_{0}^{\varepsilon})
is bounded uniformly with respect to
\varepsilon
, see (
27
) and (
36
). We add technical conditions which are distinct when
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{3}
(Subsection
2.3
) and when
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(Subsection
2.4
) to guarantee that the difference
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}-1
vanishes in
L^{\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
when
\varepsilon
goes to zero. Then,
up to a subsequence and at least in a weak sense (specified further), we have

\varrho^{\epsilon}_{0}:=\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}-1}{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}\varrho_{0}\,,\qquad v^{\varepsilon}_{0}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}v_{0}\in L^{2}\,,\qquad B^{\varepsilon}_{0}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}B_{0}\in L^{2}\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0_{+}\,.

We will show (Theorems
1
and
2
) that, always up to a subsequence, the difference
\rho^{\varepsilon}-1
vanishes strongly in
L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{\gamma}_{\rm loc}(\Omega))
and that, at least in a weak sense, we have
1
1

1

In the periodic case, let
\overline{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}
be the constant (close to
1
) defined by (
28
). When
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{3}
,
as stated in Theorem
1
, the definition of
\varrho^{\epsilon}
should be replaced by
\varrho^{\epsilon}:=(\rho^{\varepsilon}-\overline{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}})/\varepsilon
.

\varrho^{\epsilon}:=\frac{\rho^{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}\varrho\,,\qquad v^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}v\,,\qquad B^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}B\,,\quad\mbox{as}\quad\varepsilon\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0_{+}\,.

The next stage is to identify the limit
(\varrho,v,B)
.

1.2.
The RMHD model

The perpendicular component
B_{\perp}:={}^{t}(B_{1},B_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of
B
and the perpendicular component
v_{\perp}:={}^{t}(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of
v
can be identified independently by solving the following nonlinear closed system

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}B_{\perp}-\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v_{\perp}+\nabla_{\perp}\cdot(B_{\perp}\otimes v_{\perp}-v_{\perp}\otimes B_{\perp})-\eta_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}B_{\perp}-\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B_{\perp}=0\,,\\
&\partial_{t}v_{\perp}-\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B_{\perp}+\nabla_{\perp}\cdot(v_{\perp}\otimes v_{\perp}-B_{\perp}\otimes B_{\perp})+\nabla_{\perp}\uppi-\mu_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}v_{\perp}-\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v_{\perp}=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

(12)

together with the (transverse velocity) divergence-free condition

\nabla_{\perp}\cdot v_{\perp}=0\,,

(13)

and the initial data

(B_{\perp},v_{\perp})_{\mid t=0}=({\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}B_{0\perp},{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{0\perp})\in L^{2}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{4})\,,

(14)

where the projection
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}
denotes the (two-dimensional) transverse Leray operator. Passing to the weak limit in
\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot B_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0
, we can easily infer that
\nabla_{\perp}\cdot B_{0\perp}=0
, and therefore
B_{0\perp}={\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}B_{0\perp}
. The same applies concerning
v_{0}^{\varepsilon}
in the case of prepared data. For unprepared data
v_{0}^{\varepsilon}
, in general,we find that
v_{0\perp}\not={\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{0\perp}
. Still, we will show in Section
4.5
that the limit initial condition is
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{0\perp}
and not
v_{0\perp}
. This passage from
v_{0\perp}
to
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{0\perp}
reveals the underlying presence of a time boundary layer (which may arise in the absence of preparation). In the second equation of (
12
), the pressure
\uppi
plays the role of a Lagrange multiplier to ensure the transverse incompressibility of the flow. Since

\nabla_{\perp}\cdot(B_{\perp}\otimes v_{\perp}-v_{\perp}\otimes B_{\perp})={}^{t}\bigl(\partial_{2}(B_{1}\,v_{2}-v_{1}\,B_{2}),-\partial_{1}(B_{1}\,v_{2}-v_{1}\,B_{2})\bigr)\,,

exploiting (
13
), we can assert that

\partial_{t}(\nabla_{\perp}\cdot B_{\perp})-\eta_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}(\nabla_{\perp}\cdot B_{\perp})-\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}(\nabla_{\perp}\cdot B_{\perp})=0\,.

It follows that the divergence-free condition on
B_{0\perp}
is propagated. Retain that

\nabla_{\perp}\cdot B_{\perp}=0\,.

(15)

The existence of global-in-time weak solutions to (
12
)-(
13
)-(
14
) can be obtained from classical methods in
[
14
,
41
]
. Note that it can be deduced indirectly from the existence (for all
\varepsilon>0
) of weak solutions to (
3
). Indeed, as will be seen, the rigorous justification of the passage to the limit (
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
) in the system (
3
) provides another way to construct global weak solutions of (
12
)-(
13
)-(
14
). Observe also that the system is linear in the parallel direction. Thus, these global existence results remain true even when
\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0
and
\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0
.

Now, the treatment of the parallel components
(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
differs completely from what is done usually. This is due to the (unconventional) anisotropic context which forces to look at
(B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
separately. On the one hand,
(B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
appears as a leading order term, and therefore its weak limit
(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
must contribute to the main description of the flow. As such, it must be incorporated in the RMHD model. On the other hand, to some extent,
(B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
is (partly) dealt in the equations as a second order term. It follows that the determination of its weak limit
(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
is decoupled from the one of
(B_{\perp},v_{\perp})
. In fact, knowing the content of
(B_{\perp},v_{\perp})
, with the constant
\mathbbl{c}:=1+(1/b)>1
, we have access to
(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
through

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathbbl{c}\bigl(\partial_{t}B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+(v_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\bigr)-\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-(B_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-\eta_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0\,,\\
&\partial_{t}v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+(v_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-(B_{\perp}\cdot\nabla_{\perp})B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-\mu_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

(16)

and the initial data

(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})_{\mid t=0}=(\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}},v_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}})\,,\qquad\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}:=(B_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}-\varrho_{0})/\mathbbl{c}\,.

(17)

This is the viscous version of a symmetric linear system implying the known variable coefficients
v_{\perp}
and
B_{\perp}
. For smooth data
v_{\perp}
and
B_{\perp}
, the global existence is obvious. Moreover, due to (
13
) and (
15
), usual energy estimates concerning
(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
do apply without consuming any regularity on
v_{\perp}
and
B_{\perp}
. It follows that global solutions do exist even when the coefficients
v_{\perp}
and
B_{\perp}
are issued from the weak solution
(v_{\perp},B_{\perp})
in
L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},\dot{H}^{1}(\Omega))
to (
12
).

Given
\varepsilon>0
, the fluid is slightly compressible since
\rho^{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon\,\varrho^{\varepsilon}
with
\varrho^{\varepsilon}\sim\varrho
. The expression
\varrho^{\varepsilon}
(and its weak limit
\varrho
) plays at the level of (
3
) the part of a one order corrector which keeps track of the original compressibility. Now, looking at (
6
), it acts in the equations with the same order as the components
v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
and
B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
. It is therefore reasonable to find a link between
\varrho
,
v_{\perp}
and
B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
. In view of the second relation inside (
7
), we can already infer that
\mathbbmssit{p}+B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0
, where
\mathbbmssit{p}:=b\,\varrho
is the weak limit of
\mathbbmssit{p}^{\varepsilon}
. By this way,
B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
acquires asymptotically the status of a pressure which can serve to measure some compressibility in the parallel direction. For prepared data, that is when
B_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}+b\varrho_{0}=0
, we start with
\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}=B_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}
. Otherwise, for general unprepared data (which is our framework), we find that
\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}\not\equiv B_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}
, see Subsection
4.7
. Again, this is the hallmark of a boundary layer occuring at time
t=0
concerning the component
B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
.

1.3.
Global overview

This paper is devoted to the rigorous justification of the convergence of the global weak solutions to (
3
) to those of (
12
)-(
17
). As already mentioned, the nature of the singular limit depends on many factors.

1.3.1.
Preceding results

The hyperbolic version of system (
3
), which is obtained by removing viscosities and resistivities, falls into the framework of the theory of singular limits of quasilinear hyperbolic systems with large parameters. This approach is restricted to smooth solutions (say
H^{s}
with
s
large enough). It was originally developped by Klainerman and Majda
[
30
,
34
]
. In these circumstances, retain that:

•

In the smooth prepared setting, as a corollary of Theorem 3 in
[
30
]
, a convergence result does exist
[
21
]
concerning (
3
). It holds as long as the solution of the limit equations remains smooth. In a related framework, namely with a strong constant magnetic field but without spatial anisotropy, the authors of
[
25
]
study the singular limit of the local-in-time smooth solution of the ideal MHD on a bounded domain, with convenient boundary conditions and prepared initial data. It turns out that the limit system in
[
25
]
is essentially two-dimensional since the spatial variable
x_{3}
plays the role of a label (no differential nor integral operator with respect to
x_{3}
). In
[
2
]
, this method is successfully applied to the (more complex) XMHD system. Note also that we can appeal to Theorem 4 in
[
30
]
(dealing with the diffusive version of Theorem 3 in
[
30
]
) to justify the strong convergence on a fixed time interval of smooth prepared solutions to (
3
) to those of (
12
)-(
17
).

•

In the smooth unprepared setting, one possible strategy
[
42
]
is first to exhibit a smooth (in its arguments) limit profile with a double number of variables (one set representing slow variations, the other set fast ones) satisfying an appropriate limit equation (called the modulation equation). Second, it is to prove that the smooth solution of the original system converges in a strong sense on a uniform time interval to this profile evaluated at the slow and fast variables.

Weak solutions can also be considered, provided that parabolic contributions are incorporated. This allows to relax the regularity conditions, to reach all times, and therefore to reinforce the universality of reduced models. A way to make progress in this direction has been initiated in
[
33
]
which (for unprepared data) exploits the unitary group method
[
20
,
42
]
and compactness arguments to construct a filtered profile for the irrotational part of the velocity field. From this filtered profile, the authors of
[
33
]
construct a sequence of approximations to the limit solution. Then, they exploit this sequence to pass to the limit in the nonlinear terms. By doing so, they observe that the solenoidal part of the velocity field inherits a strong convergence, while only weak convergence results are available concerning the irrotational part.

The discussion is very sensitive to the type of domain:
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
or
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. In the case of the whole space, the proof of
[
33
]
has been simplified in
[
11
]
by using Strichartz estimates
[
17
,
27
]
. This allows to improve the convergence result of the irrotational part of the velocity field, which is precisely the part containing the rapid oscillating acoustic waves. Indeed, the authors of
[
11
]
remark that this irrotational part satisfies a linear (isotropic) wave equation. From there, due to dispersive effects (in all spatial variables), it must asymptotically vanish in a strong sense.

1.3.2.
The anisotropic complications

We clarify here the important unsolved specificities induced by the implementation of distinct spatial scales. In the smooth prepared context, new problems already arise. For instance, as observed in
[
2
]
, the anisotropy can preclude obtaining a complete WKB expansion. Even in the smooth (prepared or not) case, the particularities related to the asymptotic study of (
3
) have not yet been explored. Inspired by
[
33
]
, our aim is to go directly to weak solutions. We consider viscous and resistive situations vs. (almost) hyperbolic; global weak solutions vs. local strong solutions;
L^{p}
and periodic solutions vs. Sobolev solutions; and general data vs. prepared data. In so doing, the smooth strategies do not help. The good benchmark is
[
33
]
. But MHD equations are quite different from compressible fluid equations
[
33
]
. And thus, the discussion must be adapted to cover the magnetic effects. There are many important challenges to elucidate, especially:

•

The unitary group method involves
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\perp}v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
and
b\,\varrho^{\varepsilon}+B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
. It allows to filter out fast oscillating magnetosonic waves propagating in the transverse directions in ways that have not yet been investigated (even in the smooth context). Note in particular that
b\,\varrho+B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0
, instead of simply
\varrho=0
in
[
33
]
.

•

The nonlinear expressions involving
B^{\varepsilon}
are, of course, absent in
[
33
]
.

•

Even the tensor product
\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}\otimes v^{\varepsilon}
must be dealt differently. Indeed, in our setting, both
v^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
are left aside by the filtering. Other arguments must be introduced to understand what happens at the level of
v^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
and
B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
, that is how to recover (
16
). To deal with this issue, we exploit particular cancellations provided by the structure of system (
3
) that we combine with some compactness results developed in
[
32
]
in order to prove the existence of weak solutions to the compressible Navier–Stokes equations. Indeed, in particular from (
6
), we observe that
\partial_{t}(B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-\varrho^{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{O}(1)
. Then, the quantity
(B^{\varepsilon}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}-\varrho^{\varepsilon})
will yield the right unknown to prove the limit equation for
B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
. Moreover, in the case of the whole space, in contrast to
[
11
]
, we cannot exploit (isotropic) Strichartz estimates to obtain the strong convergence of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\perp}v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
, since the resulting wave equation is posed only in the perpendicular spatial variables
x_{\perp}
, the parallel spatial variable
x_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
being seen as a continuous label. Therefore, a natural and interesting open question arises: could only transverse dispersive effects (and thus some kind of anisotropic Strichartz’s estimates) be used to show that
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\perp}v^{\varepsilon}_{\perp}
vanishes strongly ? This question will be addressed in further work.

1.3.3.
Plan of the work

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we state our main results. In Section
3
, we come back to the physical motivations and to the origin of our anisotropic scaling. In Sections
4
and
5
, we prove the convergence of the compressible MHD equations (
3
)-(
4
) to the RMHD equations (
12
)-(
13
)-(
16
). We start in Section
4
with the case of a periodic domain. Then, in Section
5
, we perform this investigation in the whole space. Finally, in Appendix
A
, we recall functional analysis results which are exploited throughout the paper.

2.
Main results

In Subsection
2.1
, we specify some notations. In Subsection
2.2
, we
recall the notion of weak solutions. In Subsection
2.3
, we state our main results in the case of
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
. In Subsection
2.4
, we do the same for
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

2.1.
Notation

Let
\Omega
be either the periodic domain
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
or the whole space
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. For
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
, we shall use the standard non-homogeneous Sobolev spaces

W^{s,p}(\Omega)=({\rm I}-\Delta)^{-s/2}L^{p}(\Omega)\,,\qquad H^{s}(\Omega)=W^{s,2}(\Omega)\,,

and their homogeneous versions

\dot{W}^{s,p}(\Omega)=(-\Delta)^{-s/2}L^{p}(\Omega)\,,\qquad\dot{H}^{s}(\Omega)=\dot{W}^{s,2}(\Omega)\,.

We introduce the transverse Leray projection operator
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}:W^{s,p}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})\rightarrow W^{s,p}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
onto vector fields which are divergence-free in the perpendicular direction

v_{\perp}={\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}+{\mathbb{Q}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}\,,\qquad\nabla_{\perp}\cdot({\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{\perp})=0\,,\qquad\nabla_{\perp}\times({\mathbb{Q}}_{\perp}v_{\perp})=0\,,\qquad\forall v_{\perp}\in L^{2}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})\,,

where
\nabla_{\perp}\times v_{\perp}:=\partial_{1}v_{2}-\partial_{2}v_{1}
. From the Mikhlin–Hörmander Fourier multipliers theorem, the operators
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\perp}
are continuous maps from the Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
into itself for
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
1<p<\infty
. In addition
[
33
]
, for all
\delta>0
, we have the following continuous embedding
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}(L^{1}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow W^{-\delta,1}(\Omega)
. This embedding can be justified simply by observing that on the one hand the operators
{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\perp}
are continuous maps from
L^{1}(\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
into the Lorentz space
L^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
(or weak
L^{1}(\Omega)
; see, e.g., Theorem 5.3.3 in
[
19
]
) and on the other hand the continuous embedding
L^{1,\infty}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{-\delta,1}(\Omega)
holds. We denote by
\mathscr{C}(0,T;L_{\rm weak}^{p}(\Omega))
, the space of functions which are continuous with respect to
t\in[0,T]
, with values in
L^{p}(\Omega)
, with the weak topology. Moreover, we introduce the differential operator
D_{\varepsilon}\equiv{}^{t}\nabla_{\varepsilon}
. The scalar product between two matrices
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
is defined as
M_{1}:M_{2}=\sum_{ij}M_{1ij}M_{2ij}
. Moreover, given two vectors
B_{\perp}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and
v_{\perp}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, we adopt below the convention
B_{\perp}\times v_{\perp}:=B_{1}\,v_{2}-B_{2}\,v_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

2.2.
Weak solutions

Weak solutions of RMHD equations will be recovered by passing to the limit (
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}
) in the weak formulation associated with (
3
)-(
4
). It is therefore important to specify what is meant by a weak solution to (
3
)-(
4
) and to (
12
)-(
13
)-(
16
) when
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{3}
and when
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Given initial data as in (
8
), with

\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{1}(\Omega)\,,\quad\ \ v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,,\,B_{0}^{\varepsilon},\,\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(\Omega)\,,\quad\ \ \nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot B_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0\ \,\mbox{in }\ \mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)\,,

(18)

a triplet
(\rho^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon})
satisfying

\rho^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm loc}^{\gamma}(\Omega))\,,\qquad(v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon},\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}v^{\varepsilon})\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}(\Omega))\,,

(19)

is said to be a weak solution of (
3
)-(
4
) if for all
\psi={}^{t}({}^{t}\psi_{\perp},\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
and for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}})
with
p^{\varepsilon}=a\,(\rho^{\varepsilon})^{\gamma}
, we have

\int_{\Omega}dx\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\varphi(0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{\Omega}dx\,\rho^{\varepsilon}\big(\partial_{t}\varphi+v^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{\varepsilon}\varphi\big)=0\,,

(20)

\int_{\Omega}dx\,\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}v_{0\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\psi_{\perp}(0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{\Omega}dx\,\bigg(\rho^{\varepsilon}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\psi_{\perp}+\big(\rho^{\varepsilon}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\otimes v^{\varepsilon}-B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\otimes B^{\varepsilon}\big):D_{\varepsilon}\psi_{\perp}\\
+\bigg\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}p^{\varepsilon}+\frac{B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{|B^{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{2}\bigg\}\nabla_{\perp}\cdot\psi_{\perp}-B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\perp}+\mu_{\perp}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\psi_{\perp}+\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\perp}\bigg)=0\,,

(21)

\int_{\Omega}dx\,\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}v_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}(0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{\Omega}dx\,\bigg(\rho^{\varepsilon}v_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\big(\rho^{\varepsilon}v_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\otimes v^{\varepsilon}-B_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\otimes B^{\varepsilon}\big):D_{\varepsilon}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\\
+\bigg\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}p^{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\frac{|B^{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{2}\bigg\}\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\mu_{\perp}v_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\perp}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\varepsilon\lambda^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})\bigg)=0\,,

(22)

\int_{\Omega}dx\,B_{0\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\psi_{\perp}(0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{\Omega}dx\,\bigg(B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\psi_{\perp}-v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\perp}-(B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\times v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon})\nabla_{\perp}\times\psi_{\perp}\\
+\varepsilon v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\perp}-\varepsilon B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\perp}+\eta_{\perp}B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\Delta_{\perp}\psi_{\perp}+\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\perp}\bigg)=0\,,

(23)

\int_{\Omega}dx\,B_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}(0)+\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\int_{\Omega}dx\bigg(B_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\\
-v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{\perp}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\eta_{\perp}B_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\perp}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B_{{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}^{\varepsilon}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\psi_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\bigg)=0\,,

(24)

\int_{\Omega}dx\,B^{\varepsilon}(t)\cdot\nabla_{\varepsilon}\varphi(t)=0\,,\quad\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\,.

(25)

The notion of weak solution to (
12
)-(
13
) is obtained by testing (
12
) against all
\psi_{\perp}\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\Omega;{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
which are such that
\nabla_{\perp}\cdot\psi_{\perp}=0
. Concerning (
16
), it suffices to select scalar functions.

2.3.
The periodic case

This is when
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{3}
. The functional framework is based on
[
23
,
33
]
. Select initial data satisfying

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho^{\varepsilon}_{|_{t=0}}=\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{\gamma}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\,,\qquad\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\geq 0\,,\qquad\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\,,\\
&(\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon})_{|_{t=0}}=m_{0}^{\varepsilon}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}&\mbox{if }\ \rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\neq 0\\
&0&\mbox{if }\ \rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0\end{aligned}\right\}\in L^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\,,\\
&B^{\varepsilon}_{|_{t=0}}=B_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\,,\qquad\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot B_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\qquad\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}dx\,B_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(26)

We assume that these regularity assumptions are uniform with respect to
\varepsilon
. Furthermore, given a constant
C_{0}
(not depending on
\varepsilon
), we impose

\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}dx\,\big(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|v_{0}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|B_{0}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\big)+\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{2}(\gamma-1)}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}dx\,\big((\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})^{\gamma}-\gamma\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}(\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}})^{\gamma-1}+(\gamma-1)(\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}_{0}})^{\gamma}\big)\leq C_{0}\,.

(27)

This is completed by

\overline{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}:=\frac{1}{|{\mathbb{T}}^{3}|}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}dx\,\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}1\,,\ \mbox{ as }\varepsilon\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0_{+}\,.

(28)

This bound gives access to weak compactness. Modulo the extraction of subsequences (which are not specified), we can say that
\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
B_{0}^{\varepsilon}
converge weakly in
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})
to
u_{0}
and
B_{0}
respectively. From (
27
), some information on
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
\varrho_{0}^{\varepsilon}:=(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}-\overline{\varrho_{0}^{\varepsilon}})/\varepsilon
can also be extracted. We will first show (see the proof of Lemma
1
) that
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1
in
L^{\gamma}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})
–strong. Then, we will see that
\varrho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\varrho_{0}
in
L^{\kappa}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})
–weak for
\kappa:=\min\{2,\gamma\}
. Using
\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup u_{0}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})
–weak and
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1
in
L^{\gamma}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})
–strong, we obtain
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup u_{0}=v_{0}
in
L^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})
–weak.

As soon as
\gamma>3/2
, the contribution
[
23
]
furnishes a weak solution to (
3
)-(
4
) with

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}\bigl({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{\gamma}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\bigr)\,,\quad v^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{2}\bigl({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\bigr)\,,\\
&\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}v^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}\bigl({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\bigr)\,,\quad\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}\bigl({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\big)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\rm loc}\big({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm weak}^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\big)\,,\\
&B^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}\bigl({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\bigr)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\rm loc}\bigl({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm weak}^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\bigr)\cap L_{\rm loc}^{2}\bigl({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\bigr)\,,\qquad\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}dx\,B^{\varepsilon}=0\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(29)

The mass is conserved

\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}}:=\frac{1}{|{\mathbb{T}}^{3}|}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}dx\,\rho^{\varepsilon}=\overline{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\,,

and thus, using (
28
), we deduce that
\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}}\rightarrow 1
, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}
. Moreover, we have two energy inequalities

\mathbbm{E}^{\varepsilon}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}ds\,\mathbbm{D}^{\varepsilon}(s)\leq\mathbbm{E}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,,\ \mbox{ a.e. }t\in[0,+\infty)\,,

(30)

with
\mathbbm{E}\in\{\mathcal{E}_{1},\,\mathcal{E}_{2}\}
, where for
i=1,2
,

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{\varepsilon}(t)=\int_{\Omega}dx\,\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{\varepsilon}|v^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|B^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\Pi_{i}(\rho^{\varepsilon})\Big)\,,\quad\mathcal{E}_{i0}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\Omega}dx\,\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|v_{0}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}|B_{0}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\Pi_{i}(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})\Big)\,,

(31)

\mathbbm{D}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{\Omega}dx\,\big(\mu_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}|\nabla_{\perp}v^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}|\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}v^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\lambda^{\varepsilon}|\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot v^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\eta_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}|\nabla_{\perp}B^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}|\partial_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}B^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\big)\,,

(32)

and

\Pi_{1}(\rho^{\varepsilon})=\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{2}(\gamma-1)}(\rho^{\varepsilon})^{\gamma}\,,\quad\ \Pi_{2}(\rho^{\varepsilon})=\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{2}(\gamma-1)}\big((\rho^{\varepsilon})^{\gamma}-\gamma\rho^{\varepsilon}(\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}})^{\gamma-1}+(\gamma-1)(\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}})^{\gamma}\big)\,.

(33)

For
i=1
, the inequality (
30
) is a consequence of straightforward calculation involving (
3
). Using the mass conservation, we can check that the inequality (
30
) for
i=2
is equivalent to (
30
) for
i=1
. The case
i=2
is introduced because it allows a better comparison of
\rho^{\varepsilon}
with
\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}}
.

Theorem 1

(Convergence of MHD to RMHD on a periodic domain)
.

Assume
\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{3}
and
\gamma>3/2
. Consider a sequence
\{(\rho^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon>0}
of weak solutions to the compressible MHD system (
3
)-(
4
) with initial data
\{(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon},v_{0}^{\varepsilon},B_{0}^{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon>0}
as in (
27
). Let us set
\varrho^{\varepsilon}:=(\rho^{\varepsilon}-\overline{\rho^{\varepsilon}})/\varepsilon
. Then, up to a subsequence, the family
\{(\rho^{\varepsilon},\varrho^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon>0}
converges to
(1,\varrho,v,B)
as indicated below

\displaystyle\rho^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}1\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{\gamma}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm strong}\,,

\displaystyle\varrho^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}\varrho\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{\kappa}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak--}\ast\,,\quad\kappa=\min\{2,\gamma\}\,,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{\displaystyle P}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}=v_{\perp}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{p}\cap H^{s}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm strong}\,,\quad 1\leq p<6\,,\quad 0\leq s<1\,,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{\displaystyle Q}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}0\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak}\,,

\displaystyle v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak}\,,

\displaystyle B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}B_{\perp}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{r}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm strong}\,,\quad 1\leq r<\infty\,,

\displaystyle B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak}\,\cap\,L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak--}\!\ast\,.

The limit point
(v,B)
is a weak solution to the RMHD equations (
12
)-(
13
)-(
16
) with initial data

(B_{\perp},v_{\perp})_{|_{t=0}}=(B_{0\perp},{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{0\perp})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\,,\qquad(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})_{|_{t=0}}=(\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}},v_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3})\,,

where
\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}
is as in (
17
), and it satisfies the following regularity properties

B\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\cap L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\,,\qquad v\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\cap L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\,.

Moreover, the components
\varrho
and
B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
are linked together by the relation
b\,\varrho+B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0
, for a.e.
(t,x)\in]0,+\infty[\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
.

2.4.
The whole space case

This is when
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. In order to define weak solutions in the whole space, we need to introduce the following special type of Orlicz spaces
L_{q}^{p}(\Omega)
(see Appendix A of
[
32
]
for more details on these spaces),

L_{q}^{p}(\Omega)=\big\{f\in L_{\rm loc}^{1}(\Omega)\ \big|\ f\mathbbm{1}_{\{|f|\leq\delta\}}\in L^{q}(\Omega),\ \ f\mathbbm{1}_{\{|f|>\delta\}}\in L^{p}(\Omega),\ \delta>0\big\}\,,

(34)

where the function
\mathbbm{1}_{S}
denotes the indicator function of the set
S
. Obviously
L_{p}^{p}(\Omega)\equiv L^{p}(\Omega)
.

The functional framework is based on
[
23
,
33
]
. Select initial data satisfying

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho^{\varepsilon}_{|_{t=0}}=\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,\quad\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}-1\in L_{2}^{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,\quad\gamma>3/2\,,\quad\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\geq 0\,,\quad\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,\\
&(\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon})_{|_{t=0}}=m_{0}^{\varepsilon}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}&\mbox{if }\ \rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\neq 0\\
&0&\mbox{if }\ \rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0\end{aligned}\right\}\in L_{\rm loc}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,\\
&B^{\varepsilon}_{|_{t=0}}=B_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,\qquad\nabla_{\varepsilon}\cdot B_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\qquad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}dx\,B_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\\
&\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}1\,,\quad v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0\,,\quad B_{0}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0\,,\quad\mbox{as }|x|\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\infty\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(35)

We assume that these regularity assumptions are uniform with respect to
\varepsilon
. Furthermore, given a constant
C_{0}
(not depending on
\varepsilon
), we impose

\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}dx\,\big(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}|v_{0}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}+|B_{0}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\big)+\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{2}(\gamma-1)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}dx\,\big((\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon})^{\gamma}-\gamma\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}+\gamma-1\big)\leq C_{0}\,.

(36)

This bound gives access to weak compactness. Modulo the extraction of subsequences (which are not specified) we can say that
\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
B_{0}^{\varepsilon}
converge weakly in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
to
u_{0}
and
B_{0}
respectively. From (
27
), we will show (see the proof of Lemma
6
) the subsequent results. First, we will obtain (uniformly in
\varepsilon
) the bounds
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
,
\varrho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L_{2}^{\kappa}\cap L_{\rm loc}^{\kappa}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
, with
\kappa=\min\{2,\gamma\}
, as well as
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
. Second, we will obtain
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1
in
L_{2}^{\gamma}\cap L_{\rm loc}^{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
–strong, and
\varrho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\varrho_{0}
in
L_{\rm loc}^{\kappa}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
–weak. Moreover, using
\sqrt{\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup u_{0}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
–weak and
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1
in
L_{\rm loc}^{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
–strong, we obtain
\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon}v_{0}^{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup u_{0}=v_{0}
in
L_{\rm loc}^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})
–weak.

As soon as
\gamma>3/2
, the contribution
[
23
]
furnishes a weak solution to (
3
)-(
4
) with

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\rho^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm loc}^{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\,,\quad\rho^{\varepsilon}-1\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{2}^{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\,,\quad\nabla v^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\,,\\
&\sqrt{\rho^{\varepsilon}}v^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\,,\quad\rho^{\varepsilon}v^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}\big({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm loc}^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\big)\cap\mathscr{C}_{\rm loc}\big({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm loc\,weak}^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\big)\,,\\
&B^{\varepsilon}\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\cap\mathscr{C}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm weak}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\cap L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\,,\quad\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}dx\,B^{\varepsilon}=0\,,\\
&\rho^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}1\,,\quad v^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0\,,\quad B^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}0\,,\quad\mbox{as }|x|\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\infty\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(37)

Moreover, we have the energy inequality (
30
) with
\mathbbm{E}^{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{E}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
given by the formula (
31
) and
\Pi_{i}=\Pi_{3}
, where
\Pi_{3}
is defined by

\Pi_{3}(\rho^{\varepsilon})=\frac{a}{\varepsilon^{2}(\gamma-1)}\big((\rho^{\varepsilon})^{\gamma}-\gamma\rho^{\varepsilon}+\gamma-1\big)\,.

(38)

This energy inequality is the consequence of straightforward calculation involving (
3
).

Theorem 2

(Convergence of MHD to RMHD on the whole space)
.

Assume
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{3}
and
\gamma>3/2
. Consider
\{(\rho^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon>0}
a sequence of weak solutions to the compressible MHD system (
3
)-(
4
) with initial data
\{(\rho_{0}^{\varepsilon},v_{0}^{\varepsilon},B_{0}^{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon>0}
as in (
36
). Let us set
\varrho^{\varepsilon}:=(\rho^{\varepsilon}-1)/\varepsilon
. Then, up to a subsequence, the family
\{(\rho^{\varepsilon},\varrho^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon})\}_{\varepsilon>0}
converge to
(1,\varrho,v,B)
as indicated below

\displaystyle\rho^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}1\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{2}^{\gamma}\cap L_{\rm loc}^{\gamma}\cap H^{-\upalpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm strong}\,,\quad\upalpha\geq 1/2\,,

\displaystyle\varrho^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}\varrho\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm loc}^{\kappa}\cap H^{-\upalpha}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak--}\ast\,,\quad\kappa=\min\{2,\gamma\}\,,\quad\upalpha\geq 1/2\,,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{\displaystyle P}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}=v_{\perp}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm loc}^{p}\cap H_{\rm loc}^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm strong}\,,\quad 1\leq p<6\,,\quad 0\leq s<1\,,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{\displaystyle Q}}_{\perp}v_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}0\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak}\,,

\displaystyle v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak}\,,

\displaystyle B_{\perp}^{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}B_{\perp}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{r}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm strong}\,,\quad 1\leq r<\infty\,,

\displaystyle B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\xrightharpoonup{\hskip 8.19447pt}B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\ \ \mbox{\rm in }\ L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak}\,\cap\,L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\!-\!\mbox{\rm weak--}\!\ast\,.

The limit point
(v,B)
is a weak solution to the RMHD equations (
12
)-(
13
)-(
16
) with initial data

(B_{\perp},v_{\perp})_{|_{t=0}}=({\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}B_{0\perp},{\mathbb{P}}_{\perp}v_{0\perp})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,\qquad(B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu},v_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})_{|_{t=0}}=(\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}},v_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3})\,,

where
\mathbbmsl{B}_{0{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}}
is as in (
17
), and it satisfies the following regularity properties

B\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\cap L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\,,\qquad v\in L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{3}))\cap L_{\rm loc}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{+};H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{3}))\,.

Moreover, the components
\varrho
and
B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
are linked together by the relation
b\,\varrho+B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=0
, for a.e.
(t,x)\in]0,+\infty[\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.

3.
Physical motivations and scaling

The dimensional magnetohydrodynamic equations reads

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\text{t}}\uprho+\nabla\cdot(\uprho\text{v})=0\,,\\
&\partial_{\text{t}}(\uprho\text{v})+\nabla\cdot(\uprho\text{v}\otimes\text{v})+\nabla\text{p}+\text{B}\times(\nabla\times\text{B})-\upmu_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}\text{v}-\upmu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\text{v}-\uplambda\nabla(\nabla\cdot\text{v})=0\,,\\
&\partial_{\text{t}}\text{B}+\nabla\times(\text{B}\times\text{v})-\upeta_{\perp}\Delta_{\perp}\text{B}-\upeta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\Delta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\text{B}=0\,,\end{aligned}\right.

(39)

with the divergence-free condition
\nabla\cdot\text{B}=0
, and the barotropic closure
\text{p}=\text{p}(\uprho)=\mathfrak{a}\uprho^{\gamma}
,
\gamma>1
. The triplet
(\uprho,\,\text{v},\,\text{B})=(\uprho,\,\text{v},\,\text{B})(\text{t},\text{x}_{\perp},\text{x}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
denotes respectively the dimensional fluid density, fluid velocity, and magnetic field. The variable
t
represents the dimensional time variable, while the two-dimensional (resp. one-dimensional) variable
\text{x}_{\perp}
(resp.
\text{x}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
) represents the perpendicular (resp. parallel) dimensional space variable.

3.1.
Large aspect ratio framework

Anisotopic plasmas with a strong background magnetic field are ubiquitus in astrophysical, space and fusion sciences. As an example, for fusion plasmas, the straight rectangular tokamak model involves a very long periodic column, whose section is a small periodic rectangle. The corresponding geometry and scalings are detailed carefully in
[
44
]
. Another example comes from various astrophysical plasmas such as the solar wind or the magnetosheath for which the underlying RMHD ordering is precisely described in
[
40
]
. In order to obtain the dimensionless MHD equations (
3
), we must first adimensionalize equations (
39
), and then choose a scaling. Putting dimensions into the “bar” quantities, we define the dimensionless unknowns and variables as
\text{t}=\bar{\text{t}}\,t
,
\text{x}_{\perp}=\bar{\text{x}}_{\perp}\,x_{\perp}
,
\text{x}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\bar{\text{x}}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\,x_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
,
\upmu_{\perp}=\bar{\upmu}_{\perp}\,\mu_{\perp}
,
\upmu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\bar{\upmu}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\,\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
,
\uplambda=\bar{\uplambda}\,\lambda
,
\upeta_{\perp}=\bar{\upeta}_{\perp}\,\eta_{\perp}
,
\upeta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\bar{\upeta}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}\,\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
,
\mathfrak{a}=\bar{\mathfrak{a}}\,a
,
\uprho=\bar{\uprho}\,\rho
,
\text{v}=\bar{\text{v}}\,v
, and
\text{B}=\bar{\text{B}}\,B
. From this, and the barotropic state law, we deduce the dimensionless pressure as
\text{p}=\bar{\text{p}}\,p
with
\bar{\text{p}}=\bar{\mathfrak{a}}\bar{\uprho}^{\gamma}
and
p=a\rho^{\gamma}
. We also define important physical quantities such as the Alfvén velocity
v_{A}:=\bar{\text{B}}/\sqrt{\bar{\uprho}}
, the sound velocity
v_{s}:=\sqrt{\gamma\bar{\text{p}}/\bar{\uprho}}
, the parameter
\beta:=\bar{\text{p}}/|\bar{\text{B}}|^{2}=v_{s}^{2}/(\gamma v_{A}^{2})
, the Alfvén number
\varepsilon_{A}:=\bar{\text{v}}/v_{A}
and the Mach number
\varepsilon_{M}:=\bar{\text{v}}/v_{s}=\varepsilon_{A}/(\sqrt{\gamma\beta})
. Here, we suppose that the parameter
\beta
is of order one, and thus we set
\beta=1
. This configuration is called the
high

\beta
ordering
[
45
]
, and often appears in space plasmas
[
3
,
29
,
37
]
. In other situations, such as plasmas of tokamaks
[
13
,
24
,
26
]
, the parameter
\beta
can be relatively small; this is the so-called
low

\beta
regim. Indeed, since
\beta
measures the ratio of the fluid pressure to the magnetic pressure, a magnetically well-confined plasmas is achieved for low
\beta
. Since here we choose
\beta=1
, we have
v_{s}=\sqrt{\gamma}v_{A}\simeq v_{A}
.

In order to understand which parts of the solution of the MHD equations (
3
) are eliminated in the reduced model (
12
)-(
17
), we now recall the different types of (linear) waves propagating in a plasma governed by the MHD equations (
39
). Dropping viscosities and resistivities terms, it is well-known
[
39
]
that the system (
39
) is hyperbolic, but not strictly hyperbolic since some eigenvalues may coincide. Linearizing the system (
39
) around the constant stationary solution
(\bar{\uprho},0,\bar{\text{B}}\,\mathbbl{b})
, where
\mathbbl{b}
is a unit vector, we obtain a linear system whose the Jacobian has real eigenvalues
[
18
]
. The set of MHD eigenvalues and associated waves can be splitted into three groups. Introducing the unit vector
\mathbbl{n}
as the direction of propagation of any wave, the sound speed
V_{s}:=\sqrt{\gamma\text{p}/\uprho}=\sqrt{a}v_{s}
(with
\rho=1
) and the Alfvén velocity
V_{A}:=|\text{B}|/\sqrt{\uprho}=v_{A}
(with
|B|=1
), these three groups are
[
18
]
:

•

Fast magnetosonic waves:

\lambda_{F}^{\pm}=\pm\,\mathcal{C}_{F}\,,\quad\mathcal{C}_{F}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(V_{s}^{2}+V_{A}^{2}+\sqrt{(V_{s}^{2}+V_{A}^{2})^{2}-4V_{s}^{2}V_{A}^{2}(\mathbbl{b}\cdot\mathbbl{n})^{2}}\Big)\,.

•

Alfvén waves:

\lambda_{A}^{\pm}=\pm\,\mathcal{C}_{A}\,,\quad\mathcal{C}_{A}^{2}=V_{A}^{2}(\mathbbl{b}\cdot\mathbbl{n})^{2}\,.

•

Slow magnetosonic waves:

\lambda_{S}^{\pm}=\pm\,\mathcal{C}_{S}\,,\quad\mathcal{C}_{S}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(V_{s}^{2}+V_{A}^{2}-\sqrt{(V_{s}^{2}+V_{A}^{2})^{2}-4V_{s}^{2}V_{A}^{2}(\mathbbl{b}\cdot\mathbbl{n})^{2}}\Big)\,.

Since here
\mathbbl{b}:=e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
, Alfvén waves cannot propagate in the perpendicular direction to
e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
. Indeed, it is well-known
[
18
]
that Alfvén waves propagate mainly along the direction (
\mathbbl{b}:=e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
) of the ambiant magnetic field. For a wave propagating in the perpendicular direction to
e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}
, we obtain
\lambda_{F}^{\pm}=\pm(V_{s}^{2}+V_{A}^{2})^{1/2}\simeq\pm V_{A}\simeq\pm V_{s}
, whereas
\lambda_{S}^{\pm}=0
. Note that in dimensionless variables we have
\lambda_{F}^{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{b+1}
, with
b=a\gamma
. Indeed, normalizing the velocity to the Alfvén velocity
v_{A}
and taking
\beta=1
in
\lambda_{F}^{\pm}=\pm v_{A}\sqrt{\beta a\gamma+1}
, we obtain the desired result. In order to understand now the nature of the waves that are filtered out from the singular part of the linear system (
6
), we rewrite it in the fast time variable
t
to obtain

\partial_{\text{t}}\varrho+\nabla_{\perp}\cdot v_{\perp}=0\,,\qquad\partial_{\text{t}}v_{\perp}+\nabla_{\perp}(b\varrho+B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})=0\,,\qquad\partial_{\text{t}}B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\nabla_{\perp}\cdot v_{\perp}=0\,.

With
U:={}^{t}(\varrho,v_{1},v_{2},B_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu})
, the previous system can be recast as
\partial_{\text{t}}U+(A_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}+A_{1}\partial_{x_{2}})U=0
, where the matrices
A_{i}
have constant coefficients depending on
b
.
With
\mathbbl{n}_{\perp}={}^{t}(\mathbbl{n}_{1},\mathbbl{n}_{2})
a unit vector in the perpendicular direction, the matrix
\mathcal{A}:=\mathbbl{n}_{1}A_{1}+\mathbbl{n}_{2}A_{2}
is diagonalizable with the real eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}(\mathcal{A})=0
(of multiplicity two),
\lambda_{+}(\mathcal{A})=\sqrt{b+1}
, and
\lambda_{-}(\mathcal{A})=-\sqrt{b+1}
. Then, the waves associated with the singular part of the linear system (
6
) are the transverse (linear) fast magnetosonic waves.

Therefore, here, we aim at filtering out the fast dynamics associated with the perpendicular fast magnetosonic waves, and keep the dynamics of waves which propagate at a speed slower than the perpendicular fast magnetosonic waves
\mathcal{C}_{F}\simeq v_{A}
. Defining the time
\uptau_{\perp}
as the time needed by a fast magnetosonic waves to cross the device in the perpendicular direction, we then have
\uptau_{\perp}v_{A}=\bar{\text{x}}_{\perp}
. Since we want to describe the dynamics on a time scale longer that
\uptau_{\perp}
, we set
\bar{\text{t}}=\uptau_{\perp}/\varepsilon
, with
\varepsilon\ll 1
. This is equivalent to describe the dynamics of waves which propagate with velocity slower than
\mathcal{C}_{F}
(or
v_{A}
). Hence, we have
\bar{\text{v}}=\varepsilon v_{A}
,
\varepsilon_{A}=\varepsilon
, and
\varepsilon_{M}=\varepsilon/(\sqrt{\gamma\beta})\simeq\varepsilon
. Moreover, we suppose a strong anisotropy between the perpendicular and parallel direction, that is
\bar{\text{x}}_{\perp}/\bar{\text{x}}_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\varepsilon
. In other words the dimensional gradient
\nabla_{\text{x}}
becomes the anisotropic dimensionless gradient
\nabla_{\varepsilon}
of (
2
). In addition, we suppose the presence of a strong constant background magnetic field in the parallel direction (
B=e_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}+\varepsilon B^{\varepsilon}
). Since
\varepsilon
is present in the resulting dimensionless system, the velocity field
v
and the density
\rho
will depend on
\varepsilon
, hence we set
v=v^{\varepsilon}
and
\rho=\rho^{\varepsilon}
. Finally, it remains to choose some scalings with respect to the small parameter
\varepsilon
for the dimensionless viscosities and resistivities. We choose
\{\mu_{\perp}=\varepsilon\mu_{\perp}^{\varepsilon},\,\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon,\,\lambda=\varepsilon\lambda^{\varepsilon}\}
, where viscosities
\{\mu_{\perp}^{\varepsilon},\,\mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon},\,\lambda^{\varepsilon}\}
satisfy (
10
), and
\{\eta_{\perp}=\varepsilon\eta_{\perp}^{\varepsilon},\,\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}=\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}/\varepsilon\}
, where resistivities
\{\eta_{\perp}^{\varepsilon},\,\eta_{\mkern 3.0mu\vphantom{\perp}\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 2.0mu\vrule depth=0.0pt\mkern 3.0mu}^{\varepsilon}\}
satisfy (
11
).

All the above considerations allows us to pass from the dimensional MHD equations (
39
) to the dimensionless ones (
3
).

3.2.
Nonlinear optics framework

Conducting fluids are traversed by electromagnetic waves, which can interact with the medium in various ways. These phenomena can be modeled by adjusting the dimensionless parameters to account for special regimes, and by incorporating (high frequency) oscillating source terms or equivalently (high frequency) oscillating initial data into the equations. Here, we choose viscosities and resistivities which accommodate the propagation of oscillating waves with wavelengths approximately
\varepsilon
. For this, we impose viscosities
